### Conflicts

1. **"8 March: 8,000 men from six states."** Not found as such. 8 Mar (Walker to Moore, La., and the others) asks only that states tender troops already in state service (OR IV-1 pp.134-135). The numbered call is **9 Mar**: places totalling 11,700 (Charleston 3,000; Pulaski 1,000; Morgan 1,000; Pensacola 5,000; New Orleans forts 700; Texas 1,000), state shares 1,000-2,000 (OR IV-1 p.135). The figure **8,000 per state** appears only after the calls of **8 Apr (3,000) and 16 Apr (5,000)**: Brown, Clark and Pickens all answer with "8,000" or "5,000 more" (pp.225-226); Alabama is stated as 5,000 "in addition to the 3,000" (p.221). Florida is 1,500 + 2,000 (pp.211, 222).
2. **"8 May: 400,000 volunteers."** The 8 May 1861 act sets no number (OR IV-1 p.302; title read exactly; JCCS pp.196-201). **400,000 volunteers for not less than twelve months nor more than three years is the act of 8 Aug 1861** (Digest sec. 114, "Aug. 8, 1861, § 1, ch. 20", read by the fetch tool). The 100 regiments appropriated on 21 May (OR IV-1 p.338) give the scale for May-July.
3. **"6 May: declaration of war."** The act of 6 May is titled as recognizing the existence of war and authorizing letters of marque (exact title, OR IV-1 p.282); no separate act of that date "declares" war. The 11 May act recites that war exists. The Digest tool read found no "6 May" act in secs. 100-188 and cites the 6 March act, a likely sign that the Digest folds the war act into marque and prize chapters not read.
4. **Letcher's proclamation date.** Printed at its end as given at Richmond **6 June** (OR I-2 p.912, fetch read); the order publishing it is **General Orders No. 25 of 8 June** (p.911 heading). Another read of p.911 called the proclamation "dated 8 June." Munford cites "June 8" in his 18 Jul letter (OR IV-1 p.487). Treat as: proclamation 6 June, published and implemented 8 June.
5. **Lee's command.** Letcher's order and Lee's G.O. 1 are both dated 23 April (OR I-2 pp.775-776); secondary accounts put the convention's confirmation and Lee's acceptance on 22 April.
6. **Terms of service.** 6 Mar act: twelve months. 28 Feb act: not less than twelve months. Walker 14 Mar says twelve months or three years (`[verify]`); Davis's 29 Apr message gives no term; from May the term is "for the war" (8 May act), with twelve-month men accepted only if armed (Walker 22-25 May, 17 Jun). Digest sec. 118 later modifies the 6 Mar act to allow "three years or during the war."
7. **Walker's "190 regiments and 34 battalions" (late July)** versus Groner's **254 regiments mustered (30 Sep)** and Davis's 35,000 in the field or moving (29 Apr): the July figure counts "accepted and ready," not mustered strength. Not reconcilable from these pages; do not convert to men without a return.
8. **OR III-5 pp.691-693** carries the heading "Union Authorities" but prints the Confederate calls and acts (28 Feb, 6 Mar, 6 May, 11 May, 9 Mar and 8 Apr calls). It is a later excerpt or compilation; OR IV-1 is authoritative. Its description of the 6 May act ("removed the numerical limit") does not match the 6 May act as read in OR IV-1; it more likely describes the 11 May act.
9. **Missouri call date.** 10 June (OR IV-1 p.375 cross-ref note) versus 12 June (NPS and secondary pages).
10. **Tennessee transfer date.** 20 Jul still negotiating (OR I-4 p.371); 26 Jul instructions (OR I-4 p.375); 31 Jul general order (snippet only); 7 Aug completion (Wikipedia only, not evidence).
11. **Page drift.** Gist's 6 Mar 8,835 note sits on p.133 by running head (not 132); the OR IV-1 page numbers in this file are within one page.

### Gaps

- Richardson vol. 1-2 (`archive.org/download/messagespapersof01conf/messagespapersof01conf_djvu.txt`) not read; Statutes at Large of the CSA (Provisional Congress) not read; the Digest covered only chars 0-200,000 of 820,858.
- JCCS pp. 204-end (11 May to 31 Aug) not read; JCCS 11 May and 21 May votes not checked. The act of 8 Aug 1861 (400,000) not read in primary text.
- **Texas**: governor not named in pages read; no return of men by 31 Jul; Committee of Public Safety ordinances and the Texas Mounted Rifles muster dates need OR I-1 (Twiggs surrender) and OR LIII/LII-II pages, and the Texas convention journals (`tarltonapps.law.utexas.edu/imgs/constitutions/files/journals1861/1861_report_1_jnl.pdf` found, not read). texashistory.unt.edu returned a validation screen (not routed around).
- **North Carolina**: the text of the act for 10,000 State troops (8 May) and of the convention ordinance of 20 May not read; counts rest on OR IV-1 pp.403-404 and secondary snippets.
- **Tennessee**: text of the 6 May act (55,000) not read; the 8 Jun vote result and Harris's 31 Jul order are not in primary text here; OR I-4 pp.363-388 (Tennessee correspondence) not read.
- **Georgia, Alabama, Mississippi, Louisiana, Florida** January-February acts (state ordinances) only as summarised in OR IV-1 pp.47-110, not read page by page; OR LII-II pp.10-26 (Alabama and Florida orders, found in cross-references) not read.
- **Arkansas** numbers by 31 Jul: no return found (only secondary "seven regiments by early June").
- **Missouri**: no OR I-3 return of the State Guard for the period.
- Blocked or refused: none of the blocked sites was needed. The shell had no network, so nothing was downloaded; a fetch-tool paraphrase carries every quotation.

### Findings outside scope

- OR IV-1 p.135: Walker's letter of 9 March tells governors to mask the movements from the US Government (OR III-5 p.691 summary), which bears on the "who knew what" model of Sumter.
- OR IV-1 pp.217, 225-227: Walker asks Pettus, Brown, Perry for troops on 8-19 April, so the Confederate machinery for calling the states ran before Lincoln's 15 April proclamation had effect.
- OR IV-1 pp.348-350, 355-358: the Walker-Brown quarrel over twelve-month versus war terms (22 May) and the arming rule (twelve-month units arm themselves) is a ready source for a state-pushback mechanism.
- OR IV-1 pp.391-392 (Letcher, 17 Jun): provisional army of 10,000 could not be raised, about one regiment.
- OR I-3 pp.609-610: Arkansas transfer articles are a usable model for dated transfer events (a "transfer" with clothing and arms terms).
- OR IV-1 p.264: Davis's 29 Apr message says some border states "seemed quite content" to levy troops against the call (paraphrase).


## Appendix H: Start loyalty and the Confederate opening (helper N8)


Status: COMPLETE (sections 0-5, Gaps, Conflicts, Findings outside scope). Quality tags: `return` = dated official count; `estimate`; `inferred`; `snippet-unverified`. The fetch tool paraphrases: figures below that I did not see as a printed table are marked "tool-read OCR" (the fetch tool read the archive.org OCR text and quoted it back; digits can be garbled). Davis's *Rise and Fall* and J. E. Johnston's *Narrative* were not used as evidence.

### 0. The repository's proposals (read from the worktree)

`control.csv` (march_1861) gives NO loyalty numbers for Virginia, North Carolina, Tennessee or Arkansas (empty shares, note "uncommitted; no sourced start control"). It carries `[I]` loyalty proposals, "waits for a loyalty field", for these (rows quoted):

- delaware: "union 650, confederate 80, neutral-contested 270"
- baltimore: "union 150, confederate 400, neutral-contested 450"
- baltimore_county, southern_maryland, frederick_md, western_maryland: "union 350, confederate 250, neutral-contested 400"
- Kentucky (all 15 regions): "union 330, confederate 250, neutral-contested 420"
- Missouri (all 14 regions): "union 450, confederate 200, neutral-contested 350"
- Texas regions: `confederate:1000` ("Texas had seceded by 5 March 1861")
- Indian Territory regions: no control; Choctaw note on the conditional 7 February resolutions.

The research note `2026-11-14-secession-winter-1861.md` ("Border states: starting control and loyalty (proposals, [I])") also proposes Virginia east of the Blue Ridge 350/300/350 and Northwest Virginia 650/100/250; these are NOT in control.csv. Method stated there: "derived from the 1860 vote and enslaved share" ([I]). Its Virginia/NW Virginia reasoning cites a "44.5/44.5 Bell-Breckinridge tie" and a "2:1 against secession in the referendum estimate" (the 23 May referendum, which is after 5 March).

### 1. 1860 presidential vote (Tribune Almanac 1861; cross-check American Presidency Project)

Primary-type source: *The Tribune Almanac and Political Register for 1861* (New York: Tribune Association), state returns printed about pp. 48-61 (index: Delaware 49, Maryland 49, Virginia 50-51, N. Carolina 48, Kentucky 52, Arkansas 53, Tennessee 54, Missouri 54-55, Texas 61). Conflicts: UCSB and the Almanac differ by tens to hundreds in Maryland (Lincoln 2,294 v 2,895), Virginia and Tennessee (Bell 69,274 vs 70,706). Wikipedia-derived pages cite Bell 74,481 / Breckinridge 74,325 for Virginia (not the Almanac). The repo note's "Bell 74,481, Breckinridge 74,325" is the Wikipedia version; the Almanac says 74,681 / 74,323.

Western Virginia counties (Almanac 1861 via W. Va. Archives): Lincoln 1,402* (county details incomplete), Bell 20,997, Breckinridge 21,908, Douglas 5,742; "Breckinridge carried West Virginia counties by 911 votes"; Virginia statewide 74,681 Bell vs 74,323 Breckinridge. So the 1860 vote does NOT show a Unionist west in the Lincoln sense: the future West Virginia counties gave Lincoln about 2.9 percent, Bell 42%, Breckinridge 44%, Douglas 12% of about 50,000. [inferred reading]. Unionism there was the Bell-Douglas conditional kind, and the secession votes of 1861 (below) are what show it.

### 3. (A) State-by-state: repository proposal v evidence v verdict v band

Permille shares are mine [inferred] from the counts in sections 1-2 (1860 totals from the Tribune Almanac 1861 as read). Reading rule: the Breckinridge share is a CEILING on committed secessionist or Southern-rights sentiment (many Breckinridge Democrats stayed loyal in 1861); the Lincoln share is a FLOOR on unconditional Unionism; Bell+Douglas+Lincoln is the non-Breckinridge (Union plus conditional) mass. The 1860 vote cannot separate "Union" from "neutral-contested"; no source I read does.

| State / region | Repository proposal | Evidence found | Verdict | Band (permille; bounding sources) |

Legislature 3 Jan 1861 "voted overwhelmingly" to stay (House Divided, citing Essah 1996 p.161; counts not seen). | **Union 650 too high; Confederate 80 too low on the vote.** The official stance (Unionist legislature) is supported; the split is not. | Unconditional-Union floor 238; non-Breckinridge 543; Southern-rights ceiling 457. No source separates committed secessionists from Breckinridge Democrats. |

### 4. (B1) The Confederate opening: dated sequence, who decided what

Everything below is from documents I read unless tagged. OR I-1 pages came from civilwar.com page images transcribed (page id = page + 150560); the fetch tool summarised each page, so quotes are short and from tool output.

| 9 April | Beauregard to Col. Pettigrew (Sullivan's Island): keep the regiment ready | Beauregard | OR I-1 p.297 | return |

### 5. (B1 cont.) The 9 April cabinet meeting: what exists

I looked for any first-hand record of the 9 April meeting at Montgomery (attendees, vote, Toombs's dissent). Result: **none found.**

- **OR I-1 pp.297-302** contain no minutes, no attendee list and no vote. The first documents after the meeting are Walker's telegrams to Beauregard (10 April, p.297), which go out under Walker's name and do not name Davis, Toombs or a vote.
- **Mary Chesnut** (*A Diary from Dixie*, 1905 ed., Charleston entries 8-15 April, pp.32-39; archive.org `adiaryfromdixie00martgoog`, tool-read): she was in Charleston; the entries name Beauregard, Pickens and her husband (who carried the demand) and say he telegraphed Davis for instructions on 12 April (p.35). Toombs, Walker and "the cabinet" do not appear in those entries. The 1905 text is a later revision [MEMOIR].
- **J. B. Jones** (*A Rebel War Clerk's Diary*, 1866; archive.org `arebelwarclerks01jonegoog`, tool-read): Jones was in Philadelphia and Baltimore on 8-10 April and reached Richmond on 12 April; his first sighting of Toombs is 22 May; no cabinet decision is described. He is not a witness.
- **Reagan, *Memoirs* (1906)** [MEMOIR, weak] (archive.org `secessioncivilwar00reagrich`, characters 100,000-350,000 read): no account of a cabinet decision on Sumter in that span (he mentions the 13 April "conflict at Charleston Harbor" as peace-hope context on p.112 and Sumter "wakened the dogs of war" on p.136). So Reagan (in that span) is not the source of the Toombs remark; chars 0-100,000 and 350,000 onward unread.
- **Walker's own statement**: Phillips (1913) footnotes "L. P. Walker's letter to Crawford" in S. W. Crawford, *Genesis of the Civil War* (1887) p.421, for a point about Toombs and the firing on the fort. I could not read that page (the archive.org scans of Crawford stop at about p.316-319 in the tool's reading). needs-source. The Encyclopedia of Alabama (snippet) says Walker later denied the "Faneuil Hall" flag remark.
- **Beauregard** was not at Montgomery; his dated notes are the OR pages above.
- **Contemporary press**: I did not read a Montgomery paper for 9-12 April. needs-source.

Where "5-1, Toombs dissenting, 9 April" comes from: the repo note cites a blog item `[CWM-Apr9]`. The Tulane "And the War Came" commentary (read) dates Davis's decision to **10 April** (not 9 April), says he decided with his cabinet's concurrence, names Toombs as the only recorded dissenter and gives no tally. It cites Nevins, *War for the Union* 1: 68-73; Current, "Confederates and the First Shot," pp.357-69; Current, *Lincoln and the First Shot*, pp.149-51, 205-6; McWhiney, pp.10-14; and Davis, *Rise and Fall* 1: 291-92 [BANNED-AS-EVIDENCE: attributed view only]. A blog (wbts-calendar, dated 10 April) says "all but one concur." Neither the date 9 April nor the tally 5-1 rests on any document I read: **unsupported; the vote count is an inference from "Toombs alone dissented."** The members present are not documented in anything I read.

#### 5.1 The "suicide, murder" remark

The earliest non-banned print source I found is **P. A. Stovall, *Robert Toombs: Statesman, Speaker, Soldier, Sage* (New York: Cassell, 1892), chapter XXI**, read at Project Gutenberg (ebook 26069, characters ~230,000-330,000). It is narration: no source is named for the cabinet scene, no date, and no attendee except Toombs and Davis; Toombs paces, then faces the President; "President Davis overruled Secretary Toombs." The words open "Mr. President, at this time, it is suicide, murder, and will lose us every friend at the North" and go on about a hornet's nest "from mountains to ocean" and "unnecessary; ... puts us in the wrong; ... fatal" (opening only quoted; rest paraphrase). **U. B. Phillips, *Life of Robert Toombs* (1913), p.235**, repeats it and footnotes "Stovall, *Toombs*, p.226" (tool-read, archive.org `lifeofroberttoom00phil`, characters 550,000-650,000). Modern repeaters (Wikipedia's Robert Toombs page, footnote 21: M. Scroggins, *Robert Toombs*, McFarland 2011, p.134; the Tulane page) go back to these or to Davis. **No Toombs letter, Stephens text, Reagan, Jones or Chesnut passage, and no contemporary newspaper reports the remark in anything I read.** Status: `[SECONDARY, UNSOURCED]`; the repo's cast note already says it "has no first-hand source; it is not used." Nothing found changes that. A Toombs letter of 2 April 1861 to the commissioners is on the Rosenbach site (https://civilwar.rosenbach.org/?p=750): not read; needs-source.

### Gaps (needs-source)
- Toombs's opposition on 9 April: Crawford, *Genesis* (1887) p.421 (Walker's letter); Stovall p.226 as a paged text; a Montgomery newspaper of 10-12 April 1861; the Rosenbach 2 April Toombs page; Davis's message to Congress of 29 April 1861 (Richardson, *Messages and Papers of the Confederacy*): none read.
- Letcher, Harris and Rector replies to Cameron: OR III-1 pp.73-81 not read.
- Tennessee legislature, 1 May to 7 May: House and Senate Journals, extra session 1861 (tnsoshistory.com returned 403).
- Texas official canvass: E. W. Winkler, ed., *Journal of the Secession Convention of Texas* (1912); Timmons 1971.
- Virginia 4 Feb popular vote; the Library of Virginia delegate chart; Reese, ed., *Proceedings of the Virginia State Convention of 1861* (1965).
- Arkansas *Journal of Both Sessions* (1861) for 18 March and 6 May; Wooster, AHQ 13 (1954).
- Kentucky House Journal for 16 May; official 20 June returns.
- Missouri 18 Feb popular returns.
- Delaware's 3 Jan count: legislative journals.
- The Tribune Almanac 1861 scan itself: Tennessee and Arkansas rows are garbled in the OCR; the 1862 Almanac Kentucky district rows are garbled.
- Blocked or failed (via the fetch tool): https://www.ncpedia.org/secession-movement (403); https://tnsoshistory.com/chapter6 (403); https://civilwaronthewesternborder.org/node/484 (403); https://publications.iowa.gov/39759/1/The_Tribute_Almanac_1862_OCR.pdf (403); https://www.knox.edu/documents/LincolnStudies/BurlingameVol2Chap22.pdf (socket hang up). Not attempted: census.gov, HathiTrust, bioguide.

### Conflicts
- Missouri 19 March: 89-1 (Journal p.46, Wikipedia) v 98-1 (State Archives timeline; a St. Louis blog 98-1, dated 21 March). The Journal governs.
- Virginia 4 April: 90-45 (Encyclopedia Virginia; the Richmond site "45 to 90") v the brief's and spirit61's "88 to 45". Virginia 17 April: 88-55 (EV, Wikipedia) v 85-55 (e-WV 2341).
- Virginia 1860: Almanac Bell 74,681 / Breckinridge 74,323 / Douglas 16,290 v UCSB 74,701 / 74,379 / 16,292 v the repo note's 74,481 / 74,325 (Wikipedia).
- Maryland 1860: Lincoln 2,294 (Almanac) v 2,895 (UCSB).
- Tennessee 8 June: 104,905 v 47,258 (Almanac, rows do not sum) / 104,913 v 47,238 / 108,274 v 47,247 (Hurlburt, incl. camps) / 108,418 v 46,996 (snippet).
- Texas referendum: 46,153-14,747 (Wikipedia, votearchive via Timmons) v 44,317-13,020 (Handbook of Texas) v 46,129-14,697 (snippet).
- Arkansas 6 May: "69-1" v "only five opposing votes" (both Encyclopedia of Arkansas entries).
- Davis's decision date: 9 April (the repo note, a blog) v 10 April (Tulane).
- Delivery of the 11 April demand: OR I-1 p.302 "sent at 2" v the later-afternoon delivery in other accounts (not read).

### Findings outside scope
- Anderson's report (OR I-1 p.12): one man was killed and two seriously wounded during the 14 April salute; this qualifies any "no one killed" line.
- The research note's Virginia proposals depend on the 23 May referendum and on a "44.5/44.5" Bell-Breckinridge tie from the Wikipedia version of the vote; the Almanac shows a 358-vote Bell margin.
- Missouri's proposal reproduces the 1860 vote arithmetic exactly (458 / 353 / 189), which suggests it was derived mechanically, not from loyalty evidence.
- No fetched page tried to direct me to do anything.


## Appendix I: Leads on surrenders, Bull Run, Pratt Street (helper N9a)


Method note. OR pages were read through civilwar.com per-page transcriptions via a fetch tool that **paraphrases**; no quotation below is verbatim unless marked. Page numbers are the site's page labels (page id = page number + offset), which match the OR running heads except where marked. All "quality" tags: return / estimate / inferred / snippet-unverified.

---
### Lead 1. Lynde's surrender, 27 July 1861 (Fort Fillmore, New Mexico Territory)

All from OR I-4, chapter XI, "Surrender at San Augustine Springs, N. Mex.", pp. 2-17 (civilwar.com ids 153015-153030; p.N = id 153013+N).

| Fact | Value | Date | Source | Page | Quality |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lynde's report is dated from Fort Fillmore | Maj. Isaac Lynde, 7th Infantry, comdg Southern District of New Mexico; report of 26 July (first part) | 26 Jul 1861 | OR I-4 | p.4 | return (self-report) |
| Force sent against Mesilla 25 July | about 380 men: six companies 7th Infantry (one as artillery), howitzer battery, two companies of rifles; one infantry company, band, convalescents left in the post | 25 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.4 | self-report |
| Texan strength as Lynde saw it | 300-400 mounted under Baylor (deserter, 24 Jul); with a 100-man reinforcement from Fort Bliss and Mesilla citizens, "nearly 700" | 24-26 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.4 | self-report (inflated, see Baylor p.17) |
| Mesilla action | Lynde's own losses: 3 killed, 2 officers and 4 men wounded; withdrew to the post | 25 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.4 | self-report |
| Baylor's own account of Mesilla | enemy "over 500" cavalry and infantry with four guns; repulsed; enemy 3 killed, 7 wounded | 25 Jul (report dated 3 Aug) | Baylor, Report No. 8, Picacho, Mesilla Valley, 3 Aug 1861 | p.16 | self-report |
| Evacuation of Fort Fillmore | Lynde judged post indefensible against artillery (sand hills; water 1.5 miles off); evacuated 1 a.m. on 27 July, destroying property he could not carry; route to Fort Stanton via San Augustine Springs (about 20 miles; longer than reported) | 27 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.5 | self-report |
| Condition of column | heat and want of water; many men and teams exhausted near a pass in the Organ Mountains; Gibbs's company 24 hours without water; Gibbs counts 150+ infantry of the rear guard unable to rise | 27 Jul | Lynde p.6; Gibbs report of 6 Aug p.7 | pp.6-7 | self-report |
| Rear guard | Texans (Lynde: eight companies of mounted men with artillery and infantry) drove in or took the rear guard of three infantry companies, and the wagons; Lynde could parade only about 100 infantry | 27 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.6 | self-report |
| Strength at surrender (Lynde) | Mounted Rifles 95 rank and file and 2 officers; 7 companies 7th Infantry with 8 officers; no exact infantry count given | 27 Jul | Lynde, OR I-4 | p.6 | self-report |
| Strength (Baylor's closing lines) | Baylor said he could not guard "over 600" prisoners with fewer than 300 men while watching for about 240 enemy expected; so released the entire command on parole | 27 Jul (written Aug-Sep) | Baylor, OR I-4 | p.17 (the page also carries the start of a second Baylor report of 21 Sep 1861 from Dona Ana) | self-report |
| Baylor's claim of capture | "entire command": eight companies infantry, three of Mounted Rifles, four pieces of artillery; all transportation, arms, ammunition, commissary and quartermaster stores; Fort Fillmore in his possession | report 3 Aug | Baylor No. 8, OR I-4 | p.16 | self-report |
| Terms of surrender (paraphrase) | Lynde's command treated as prisoners of war; families protected from insult, private property respected; officers on parole free to choose route out of the Department of New Mexico; enlisted men disarmed and given liberty of Fort Bliss pending Van Dorn's instructions | 27 Jul | OR I-4 "Terms of surrender" (signed Lynde and Baylor) | p.7 | return (the document itself) |
| Gibbs's account of terms | Lynde agreed to surrender "without consulting his officers"; every officer protested; Texans took horses, arms and transport after the column reached Las Cruces | 27-29 Jul | Capt. Alfred Gibbs, Mounted Rifles, to Canby, 6 Aug | p.8 | self-report |
| Officers' protest (hostile witness) | nearly every officer protested; Lynde said he alone was responsible; command marched about 20 miles to Las Cruces; public property turned over 29 July; all took parole/oath 1 Aug | 27 Jul-1 Aug | Asst Surg. J. Cooper McKee, Albuquerque 16 Aug (doc. 4) | p.11 | self-report |
| Parole count (the best figure) | Released on parole 410 (11 commissioned officers and 399 enlisted); in confinement as prisoners of war 16; deserted to the enemy 26 (incl. a hospital steward); available for service, not paroled, 40 | 27 Jul 1861 | Recapitulation signed Capt. J. H. Potter, 7th Infantry | p.15 | return |
| Equipment surrendered by the Rifles | 100 horses and 2 mules surrendered, 8 horses stolen; Rifles total 100 officers and men (footnote) | 27 Jul | Gibbs to Lt A. L. Anderson | p.9 | return (company-level) |
| Paroled Rifles reach Fort Craig | three Mounted Rifles companies, paroled, 6 Aug | 6 Aug | OR I-4 | p.5 | report |
| Canby's judgement | Lynde's report "in all respects unsatisfactory" (paraphrased); Canby's transmittals dated 4 and 11 Aug | 4 Aug | Canby, OR I-4 | p.2 | return |
| Court of inquiry | Gibbs asked for a court of inquiry into the surrender and his own role on 7 Nov 1861 from Jefferson Barracks, for all officers of his command | 7 Nov 1861 | Gibbs, OR I-4 | p.9 | return (a request, not a finding) |
| Dismissal | G.O. 102, War Department, 25 Nov 1861: Lynde dropped from the rolls "by direction of the President" (paraphrase); the AG's reply to a House resolution of 4 Dec 1861 says no other officer was believed implicated | 25 Nov 1861 | AG L. Thomas endorsement, OR I-4 | p.15-16 | return |

Reading notes (inferred). Number paroled 410 plus 16 held plus 26 deserters plus 40 unparoled gives 492, compared with Baylor's "over 600" and Lynde's "about 380" sent to Mesilla; no source in these pages gives a single total for the garrison. Potter's 410 is the only dated count. Officers were paroled and the enlisted men also were released on parole at Las Cruces (Lynde p.6; McKee p.11). The prisoners held (16) and the 26 deserters are Potter's.

Still unfound for lead 1: Cullum has no entry for Lynde on the web pages reached (not tried: Heitman, Cullum on penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer, Lynde's full entry); a court of inquiry was requested but no sitting was found; the exact list of arms and guns surrendered was not found in any page read (Baylor says "four pieces of artillery", Gibbs p.8 says the Texans took "horses, arms and transportation"). The surrender terms' exact text was seen only through a paraphrase: re-read OR I-4 p.7 before using words in the pack.

The sources disagree or are silent on the first shot. No choice is made here.

| Marshal George P. Kane | met the troops near Light Street with the mayor, formed his men across the street, told them to draw revolvers and shoot any man breaking the line; "did not know anything of the origin of the rencontre"; no casualty figures | 3 May 1861 | Kane's report to the Board of Police Commissioners, reproduced in Maryland State Archives biography page MSA SC 3520-12478 | -- | self-report |

Deaths. 6th Massachusetts killed: 4 (Whitney, Ladd, Taylor of Co. D; Needham of Co. I, died about a week later) per Brown, the Boston Globe of 17 April 1911 (via Acton Memorial Library) and the Maryland State Archives pages; Jones's report: 3 per Brown. Wounded: 36 (Brown) or about 35. Civilians: 12 named by the Baltimore Police Museum (James Carr or Clark, William R. Clark, Robert W. Davis, Sebastian Gill, Patrick Griffith, John McCann, John McMahon, Francis Maloney, William Maloney, Philip S. Miles, Michael Murphy, William Reid: secondary). NPS: 8 rioters, 1 bystander, 3 soldiers killed, 24 soldiers wounded. Pennsylvania militiaman George Leisenring died about a week later in Philadelphia (17 in all). Maryland State Archives pages: 4 soldiers and 12 civilians. No Maryland State Archives exhibit with a first-shot account was reached.

---

### Gaps
- Lynde: terms text (OR I-4 p.7) seen only as a paraphrase; no court of inquiry sat on record found (Gibbs asked 7 Nov 1861); Cullum/Heitman not read; arms and guns surrendered beyond Baylor's "four pieces" not itemised.
- Camp Jackson: no contemporary list of dead; Missouri Republican (11 May 1861) and the Missouri Historical Society not reached; the NPS PDF (webarchive.library.unt.edu mirror of nps.gov/jeff) returned an empty body.
- Rich Mountain: Morris and Benham reports on Corrick's Ford not read; why Pegram's 555 and McClellan's 593 differ.
- Sumter: garrison strength; date of Gallway's death; the 11 April terms text.
- Twiggs: troop numbers; G.O. No. 5; page of each document; Fort Pickens not covered.
- Bull Run: origin of 460/1,124/1,312; Beauregard's prisoner and gun passages by page; Terry's report page; McDowell's own report on guns lost; first Union notice of Corcoran.
- Pratt Street: Hicks; the Baltimore Sun and American of 20 April 1861; Jones's pp.8-9 read through paraphrase only; the Maryland State Archives exhibit.
- Blocked or empty: none refused; fetched pages were civilwar.com per-page transcriptions through a paraphrasing tool, so every page number is the site's label.

### Conflicts
1. Camp Jackson: prisoners 689 / 669 / about 1,200; parole accepted or refused; deaths 20 or 28.
2. Rich Mountain: 555 (Pegram) / 593 (McClellan) / about 600 (OR II-3).
3. Lynde: about 380 sent to Mesilla; Baylor "over 600" prisoners; Potter's 410 paroled + 16 held + 26 deserted + 40 unparoled = 492. No source gives one garrison total.
4. Bull Run: 481/1,011/1,216 (OR, Livermore) against 460/1,124/1,312 (unsourced); prisoners 1,460 to 1,600 (Confederate, wounded included) against 1,216 missing; who took Corcoran (Radford, Chisolm, Terry).
5. Pratt Street: first shot unresolved (table above); soldiers killed 3 or 4; civilians killed 9 (NPS) or 12.

### Findings outside scope
- OR II-1 p.107 and OR II-3 p.9 show the Union's early practice: officers paroled, enlisted men released on oath (Camp Jackson, Rich Mountain), no cartel; the 9 November 1861 hostage order (OR II-3 p.739) is the first reversal. Useful for the prisoners-and-exchange topic.
- OR I-4 pp.15-16: the House resolution of 4 December 1861 on paroled officers accused of cowardice at Fort Fillmore; Lynde dropped 25 November 1861 (G.O. 102).
- Fetch-tool caveat: its summaries disagreed on Jones's report (pp.7 and 9) and on the page order of Pegram's report (pp.267-268). Confirm in print before putting any number in the pack.

---

### Lead 1: Claiborne F. Jackson

#### 1a. Reply of 17 April 1861 to Cameron (OR III-1 pp.82-83)

Read on civilwar.com page transcriptions (`.../990-union-letters-orders-reports/268732-82-...` titled "82 Series III Volume I- Serial 122 - Union Letters, Orders, Reports"; `.../268733-83-...` titled "83 Series III Volume I- Serial 122..."). | Addressee | "Honorable SIMON CAMERON, Secretary of War" | 17 Apr 1861 | same | p.82 | return |
| Opening | "SIR: Your dispatch of the 15th instant, making a call on Missouri for four regiments of men for immediate service, has been received." | 17 Apr | same | p.82 | return |

The letter as printed mentions no slavery; it speaks of the call as war on the seceded States and of the requisition as illegal. | | OR III-1 pp.82-83 | | inferred from the text read (it is a short letter; I read its opening and its close as returned by the tool; the middle, if any, was not separately returned: the tool presented p.82 as ending after the "seceded States" sentence and p.83 as beginning at "Your requisition", so the letter is two paragraphs) |

**Correction to the cast note:** the cast note (V1) quoted N&H4 p.208 as "illegal, unconstitutional, revolutionary, inhuman, and diabolical". The OR text reads "illegal, unconstitutional, and revolutionary in its object, inhuman and diabolical". N&H's wording is an elided version (conflation of the string); use the OR wording. The key phrase "Not one man will the State of Missouri furnish" matches. The pages are OR III-1 pp.82-83 as cited (the civilwar.com titles confirm 82 and 83).

Passages as extracted by the fetch tool from the OCR: "Our slave interest would be destroyed, because we would have no better right to recapture a slave found in a free State than we now have in Canada." (p.36); "The owners of slaves must either remove with them to the South or sell them" (p.36); the OCR also reads "No Southern man owning slaves would come to the frontier State". Minority report (J. T. Redd and H. Hough, pp.39-40): the people of Missouri "will never surrender their slave institutions at the bidding of any earthly power" and "ardently desire the preservation of the Union and the preservation of their slave institutions in the Union". | 9 Mar 1861 | *Journal and Proceedings of the Missouri State Convention, March 1861* (St. Louis: Knapp, 1861), archive.org `proceedings00knaprich` OCR text | pp.35-40 (running page numbers as the tool reported them; may drift a page) | return (convention journal). Note: these are the convention committee's words, not Jackson's. The committee majority was unionist and argued secession would destroy slavery in Missouri. |

**What this gives the lead:** Jackson's own slavery statements in the window are NOT found in primary text. The only quoted words are the inaugural's (via a Confederate Military History mirror): the slaveholding states' destiny "is one and the same" and Missouri should "stand by her sister slaveholding States". The 17 April reply to Cameron has nothing on slavery and rests the refusal on illegality and "unholy crusade". The cast note's `slavery_defence` for Jackson should not rest on the 17 April letter.

**Still unfound for lead 1:** the Missouri House/Senate Journal text of the inaugural (not on archive.org under searched titles: `rebellionrecordd01moor_0` is Moore's *Rebellion Record* vol. 1 (1861) and may print it; not read: needs a reader to search its documents section); the inaugural's presence in Moore not checked.

**Settlement:** 14 May 1861 is the date of the brigadier-general rank (Cullum and G.O. 64 agree). 6 May 1861 in Cullum is a brevet brigadier-general date (G.O. 64 also calls him "Brevet Brigadier General" before 14 May), not the full rank. 17 May appears only in Gould (1895) and the unverified snippet; it may be the date of commission or acceptance (the snippet's "to rank from 14 May" suggests 17 May was the commission date and 14 May the date of rank), not stated by any primary source I read. Use 14 May as the date of rank; note 17 May as a possible commission-date; do not use 6 May as the brigadier date.

**Settlement:** not a conflict: the order is dated 16 May; Harney received it on the evening of 30 May (so "last evening" in a letter of 31 May); he relinquished at once on 30 May; Lyon assumed command on 31 May. Delay of about 14 days between order and delivery (the 22 May letter shows he was still in office, evidently unaware). Harney then wrote on 31 May that he would resume command, which Lyon's G.O. 5 of the same day overrode. Page numbers from the civilwar.com titles.

| Other transcriptions | Tulane (Sumter site, 5 March page): "twenty thousand good and well-disciplined men"; civilwarnotebook blog (cites Abraham Lincoln Papers): "less than twenty thousand good and well disciplined men" | | https://www2.tulane.edu/~sumter/InitialProb/Mar5.html ; https://civilwarnotebook.blogspot.com/2016/09/major-robert-anderson-to-colonel-samuel.html | | secondary |

**Settlement:** 20,000. The OR (p.197), Seymour's separate "twenty-thousand", Scott's reply (20,000 volunteers + 5,000 regulars), Holt's own letter, and two other transcriptions agree; the single "seventy thousand" is an enclosure transcription on jfepperson.org and is an error or a transcription slip (the same page quotes Holt as saying twenty). Holt's 5 March letter itself is not printed in OR I-1 (a search summary says it is not in the OR; unverified); it is in the Lincoln Papers (LoC mal.0779200), not read.

**Settlement:** the Navy Register status for 1 January 1861 puts Du Pont in command at Philadelphia before 4 March 1861, consistent with the Fox header (31 Dec 1860). The repository row's 4 March 1861 appears to be wrong, or may be the date he took orders under the new administration (no source for that). Search summaries (not verified) also give 31 Dec 1860. The command order itself (Navy Department, Dec 1860) not read.

### Lead 4: Campbell's account of the Seward channel

Correction to the lead: the Campbell documents are NOT in OR I-1 pp.280-295 (I read pp.280, 285 and 290: they hold Beauregard, Pickens and Walker letters of 22 Mar to 8 Apr, with no mention of Campbell or Seward). They are in OR I-53 (Supplements), pp.162-163, Chapter LXV "Correspondence, Etc.-Confederate".

|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Campbell to Seward | Heading "WASHINGTON CITY, Saturday, April 13, 1861. Honorable WILLIAM H. SEWARD, Secretary of State: SIR: On the 15th of March ultimo I left with Judge Crawford, one of the commissioners of the Confederate States, ..." | 13 Apr 1861 | OR I-53 p.162 (https://civilwar.com/official-record/980-supplements/258053-162-series-i-volume-liii-serial-111-supplements.html; title "162 Series I Volume LIII- Serial 111 - Supplements") | p.162 | return (printed in the supplement; the page also holds a Davis message correcting a date in his "message of the 29th of April": the Campbell letter is printed with it) |
| Campbell's note of 15 March | "I feel entire confidence that Fort Sumter will be evacuated in the next ten days" (the OCR reads "entire entire"); he says he sent the substance to Seward the same evening | 15 Mar 1861 | OR I-53 p.162 | p.162 | return (Campbell's own report of what he left with Crawford) |
| Seward's written statement | "On the 1st of April I received from you the statement in writing, 'I am satisfied the Governor will not ...'": the OCR text on p.162 breaks here and runs onto p.163 (the OCR says "Governor"; the sense is probably "Government": check the scan) | 1 Apr 1861 | OR I-53 p.162 | p.162 | return (Campbell's quoting; Seward's own paper not read) |
| Campbell's letter of 7 April and Seward's reply | "On the 7th of April I addressed you a letter on the subject of the alarm" that the Government's preparations had created, "asked you if the assurances I had given were well or ill founded"; Seward's reply: "Faith as to Sumter fully kept; wait and see," | 7 Apr 1861 | OR I-53 p.163 (https://civilwar.com/official-record/980-supplements/258054-163-series-i-volume-liii-serial-111-supplements.html; title "163 Series I Volume LIII- Serial 111 - Supplements"; Chapter LXV) | p.163 | return (as quoted in Campbell's own letter; the 7 April letter text itself not found) |
| Notice of the supply expedition | Campbell relays: "An authorized messenger from President Lincoln informed Governor Pickens and General Beauregard that provisions will be sent to Fort Sumter peaceably, or otherwise by force" (Beauregard telegram of 8 April; the OCR spells "authroized"); Walker's reply of 10 April begins "If you have no doubt of the authorized character of the agent ..." | 8 and 10 Apr 1861 | OR I-53 p.163 | p.163 | return |
| Campbell's charge | "they have been abused and overreached" (of the commissioners), the administration's conduct "is the proximate cause of the great calamity", closing: "It is under an oppressive sense of the weight of this responsibility that I submit to you these things for your explanation." Signed "JOHN A. CAMPBELL, Associate Justice of the Supreme Court." (the fetch showed no date line and no salutation on p.163; the salutation and date are on p.162) | 13 Apr 1861 | OR I-53 p.163 | p.163 | return |

**Conflict with the cast note's plan:** the cast note expected the account at OR I-1 pp.280-295; it is at OR I-53 pp.162-163. The page numbers come from civilwar.com page titles (OR I-53 = Serial 111); OCR running heads can drift by one page.
**Not reached:** Campbell's later memoir *Reminiscences of the Peace Commission of 1861* (not read); the commissioners' own report (not read); the Lincoln Papers items on jfepperson.org (not read for this lead).

#### 4b. Seward to his wife: "There is but one vote in the Cabinet, and that is cast by the President"

| fact | value | date | source | page | quality |

| Frederick W. Seward, *Seward at Washington, as Senator and Secretary of State: a memoir of his life, with selections from his letters* (1891) | archive.org's in-book search for the phrase "cast by the President" finds it on page 560 of `sewardatwashingt01sewa`, in a passage about Cabinet decision-making during the debate over relieving Fort Sumter. The matched text (as the tool reported it) puts it in the narrative: public opinion assumes Cabinet questions are settled by vote; Seward held that "there is but one vote in the Cabinet, and that is cast by the President"; the President "asks the opinions of each of his advisers, but is under ..." The snippet contains NO words of "wife", "Mrs. Seward", "letter" or "wrote", and no date | memoir 1891; remark undated | https://archive.org/details/sewardatwashingt01sewa (volume 1; in-book search at ia800708.us.archive.org/fulltext/inside.php) | p.560 (index page; matches elsewhere in the same index agree with printed page numbers to within a few pages) | MEMOIR (son's memoir, 1891), secondary; the "to his wife" attribution is NOT found in this passage. Quote not verified verbatim beyond the fragments above |
| Sandburg | Sandburg is cited for the "wrote his wife" form: the Mr. Lincoln and Friends page gives "Seward wrote his wife within a few months of taking office: 'There is but one vote in the Cabinet, and that is cast by the President.'" with footnote 16, Carl Sandburg, *Abraham Lincoln: The War Years*, vol. II, p.300 | | https://www.mrlincolnandfriends.org/the-cabinet/william-seward/index.html | Sandburg II p.300 | secondary (a website citing a popular biography); no date |
| DTIC military study (not readable: 403) | a search summary says it dates the quote to June 1861 and adds "The President is the best of us all": unverified | | https://apps.dtic.mil/sti/tr/pdf/ADA610988.pdf (403) | | snippet-unverified |
| Primary letter | NOT FOUND. The Seward Papers at Rochester are blocked (not routed around). I did not read the text of the 1891 volume around p.560 (the tool's text cap, about 919,000 characters, stops in 1856); only the in-book search snippet | | | | needs-source |

**Settlement:** the earliest print I could confirm is the son's memoir (1891, p.560, as narrative, in the Sumter period), which attributes the saying to Seward as a view he held; the "letter to his wife" attribution comes via Sandburg (War Years II p.300) and has no date. The primary letter is not found. Treat as `[MEMOIR]`.

**Settlement:** the remark is first printed in Stovall (1892), p.226, with no named informant; nothing first-hand or contemporary was found; the longer "letter" version is unverified. Keep the cast note's decision not to use it.

### Gaps
- Jackson: the Missouri House/Senate Journal text of the January 1861 inaugural; Moore's *Rebellion Record* vol. 1 (archive.org `rebellionrecordd01moor_0`) not searched; no separate "4 January message" found.
- Custis: the 1858 Custis estate inventory (NPS page named, not fetched) would settle the 189/195/196/197 count; no court record found; Pryor, *Reading the Man*, and Freeman not accessible; cupola.gettysburg.edu returned 403 (not routed around).
- Mansfield: Heitman not read (the archive.org text the tool reached stops in the A's); Senate Executive Journal not read.
- Anderson: the original 28 February letter (No. 58) page in OR I-1 not found on pp.185-188; the figure is quoted at p.197. Holt's 5 March letter (Lincoln Papers, mal.0779200) not read as an image.
- Habeas: Basler CW 4:347 and OR II-1 not read; quod.lib.umich.edu blocked earlier.
- Du Pont: the Navy Register row match is by OCR position; the orders themselves not found.
- Seward-to-wife letter: Seward Papers at Rochester blocked (urprojects.lib.rochester.edu, sewardproject.org not touched); DTIC pdf 403; Frederick Seward p.560 text not read directly.
- Toombs: no first-hand source.

### Conflicts
- Jackson's reply: N&H p.208 quotes "illegal, unconstitutional, revolutionary, inhuman, and diabolical"; OR III-1 p.83 reads "illegal, unconstitutional, and revolutionary in its object, inhuman and diabolical".
- Custis slaves: 189 (ACWM) / about 195 (names in the deed) / 196 (NPS exhibit; Arlington House Foundation) / 197 (NPS) / "nearly 200". Court: sources disagree on whether, when and which court ruled (1858, 1859, 1862, or after April 1861); no primary record found.
- Mansfield: 6 May (Cullum, brevet), 14 May (Cullum full rank; G.O. 64), 17 May (Gould 1895; unverified snippet).
- Anderson: 20,000 (OR I-1 p.197; Holt via jfepperson; Tulane; others) versus 70,000 (jfepperson's enclosure transcription only).
- Seward quote: son's memoir (narrative, p.560) versus Sandburg (to his wife); dated "June 1861" in a snippet.
- Du Pont: Navy Register Jan 1861 and Fox header (31 Dec 1860) versus the repository row (4 Mar 1861).

### Findings outside scope
- Two search-result pages contained embedded instructions addressed to the assistant (one "switch to Spanish" in a cavac.at mirror of the Claiborne Jackson article, one telling the reader to change topic to a recipe on a page about Frances Seward). I ignored both. The cavac.at pages are Wikipedia-style mirrors and were not used.
- Search tool note: the search "summaries" paraphrase pages; every quotation above is limited to what the fetch tool returned as page text.
- civilwar.com page titles carry the printed page number, which makes OR page citations checkable (OR I-1 id = 150560 + page for the Charleston series; OR III-1 p.82 = id 268732).
- The Lyon page (ozarkscivilwar.org/archives/1800) has one wrong year; other facts on it not checked.
- Page numbers from the civilwar.com transcriptions may drift by one page from the printed running heads.



## Appendix K: Provisioning norms (helper N10)


Read this first.
- **Headline.** No *regulation* in the 1861 Army Regulations (Subsistence Department, paras 1176-1229) sets how many days or months of stores a post must keep. Nor did I find one in the Navy Regulations (1865 text, the only edition I could open). The norms below are therefore all PRACTICE (observed stocks, orders, reports), not REQUIREMENTS. The game should label every block that uses them `assumed`.
- **Method and reliability.** Every page was read through a fetch tool that summarises. Strings in quotation marks were returned by the tool as quotations, but I did not see the raw page, so treat each as "tool-quoted". Nothing here comes from memory. Shell downloads from archive.org timed out, so full texts were read only through WebFetch with offsets. Page numbers are running heads of the OCR/transcription and can drift by a page.
- **Quality codes.** `return` = a dated official count or order. `self-report` = a commander's own statement of his stock. `estimate` = a stated estimate. `inferred` = my arithmetic or reading. `secondary` = scholarly or reenactor work, a cross-check only. `snippet-unverified` = seen only in a search summary; carries no number.

### 1. Table of norms

| kind | norm (days or months) | basis | source and page | quality | limits |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Garrison post, fully stocked, early in the crisis | **about 4 months** of provisions (band 4 to 6) | PRACTICE | Sumter 26 Dec 1860: "about four months' supply of provisions" (OR I-1 p.2, Anderson to Adjutant-General; civilwar.com id 150562). Fort Taylor, Key West, ~15 Jan 1861: "four months' provisions" (OR I-1 p.343, Brannan; the printed date is "January 115"). Pickens, 4 Feb 1861: about 100 men "provisioned for six months" (ORN I-4 p.79, Cdr Walke to Toucey) | self-report (three posts, three authors) | Self-reports, not counts. Sumter's own count three months later shows the bread was the short item (see below). All three posts are Southern coastal forts (Charleston, Key West, Pensacola). No Fort Monroe, Tortugas, Pacific or Texas figure found. |
| Garrison post, counted item by item (Sumter, 1 Mar 1861) | Pork **2 1/4 months**; flour 19 days; hard bread 9 days; flour plus hard bread **28 days**; rice 37; coffee 40; sugar 80; vinegar 100; candles 90; soap 10; salt 30; beans 8 days. Whole command "twenty eight days" of bread | PRACTICE (count) | Lt N. J. Hall, inventory enclosed with Anderson to Cooper 2 Mar 1861. Lincoln Papers, LoC mal0766400. Not in the OR. Read via jfepperson.org/SumterHoltScottLincoln0305.htm | return (dated official count) | The command includes the engineer workmen. The State refused to let flour be bought. The limiting item was bread, not meat. |
| Garrison post, same, as relayed by Scott (12 Mar 1861) | bread, flour and rice "about 26 days"; pork "about forty eight days"; could hold "some forty days" | PRACTICE | Scott to Lincoln 12 Mar 1861, LoC mal.0802700; jfepperson.org/SumterScottAL0312.htm. Garrison "less than 100 men, including common laborers and musicians" | estimate (Scott's) | Does not match Hall (see Conflicts). |
| Garrison post sent by sea (Pickens reinforcement) | Order: not more than **4 months'** subsistence (Thomas to Dimick, 21 Jan 1861); Orders No. 13 of 23 Jan: **3 months'** provisions, "all that can be transported on the Brooklyn" | REQUIREMENT in the order, but capped by hold space | OR I-1 pp.352-53 (via jfepperson.org/SumterPickens0124.htm) | return (orders) | The ceiling is shipping, not a stocking rule. Company size not given. |
| Garrison post, low point | Sumter 1 Apr 1861: about a week more if the laborers leave; on short allowance to after 10 April | PRACTICE | OR I-1 p.230 (civilwar.com id 150790), seen only in a search snippet | snippet-unverified | Not carried. |
| Naval force off a post, low point | Pickens, c. March 1861: the fort's supplies "getting low", the naval forces' "not ten days' supply" | PRACTICE | OR I-1 p.364 (Vogdes, continuation of a letter from p.363; the letter's date and place were not visible on the page I read) | self-report | Place inferred to be Pickens. |
| Field force, carried on the man | **3 days'** cooked rations in the haversack (McDowell's army left the Potomac 16 Jul 1861; the 3 days "should have lasted through" 19 Jul). Orders of 20 Jul: **2 days'** cooked rations in the haversack | PRACTICE (orders) | OR I-2 p.318 (three days' rations in haversacks); OR I-2 p.337 (circular of 20 Jul; 3 days from 3 pm 16 Jul to the afternoon of 19 Jul); OR I-2 p.343 (Hawkins: "carrying three days' rations in their haversacks"); OR I-2 p.308 (Fry: "two days' cooked rations in their haversacks") | return | Hawkins and the p.337 report both say men ate or lost their rations early and many haversacks were later found discarded. No cooking gear was carried (p.343), so coffee, sugar, beans and rice were of little use. |
| Field force, in trains | 160,000 complete rations received at Centreville = **5 days'** subsistence for the army (about 32,000 men if the 5 days is read at full strength; that figure is mine, inferred). Stores plus fresh beef expected to last through 23 Jul | PRACTICE | OR I-2 p.337 (commissary report, author not named on the page) | estimate | No army strength on the page. The beef was driven on the hoof (65 and 70 head with two trains; Hawkins took 90 head, 48,600 rations). |
| Field force, wagon capacity | One wagon load is about **1,150 to 1,200 rations**: 56 wagon loads = 64,700 rations (OR I-2 p.343); 14 wagons about 17,000 rations (OR I-2 p.337) | PRACTICE | OR I-2 pp.337, 343 | return | Principal parts of the ration only. Hawkins's train took 14 wagons; the main train was 64 wagons (OR I-2 p.341), of which 10 or 12 teams could not pull half a load. |
| Field force, wagon allowance | **6 wagons to a full regiment** (4 baggage, 1 medical, 1 headquarters) | PRACTICE, stated retrospectively | Van Vliet, Chief Quartermaster, Army of the Potomac, 2 Aug 1862, OR I-11 pt.1 p.157 (page markers [157]-[159]; mirror historyofwar.org) | return, but 1862 and describes the campaign of that spring | Not a 1861 regulation. The 1861 Army Regulations (Art. XLII, paras 1064-1175, pp.159-173) give no wagons per regiment or per company; they only bar excess baggage (paras 1097-1098). |
| Field force, army-scale consumption | A 100,000-man army needs about 150 wagon loads of subsistence a day; over 500 tons a day of forage and subsistence together | PRACTICE | Van Vliet, OR I-11 pt.1 p.159 | estimate | Gives about 670 rations a load, against the 1,150-1,200 above (see Conflicts). |
| Field force, depot stock (Patterson, 9 Jul 1861) | Commissary supplies "about ten days", but not even one day's rations could be counted on; he halted after three days' advance and sent all wagons back | PRACTICE | Patterson to Townsend, Martinsburg, 9 Jul 1861 (OR I-2; page not shown on the spirit61.info copy) | self-report | Page number needed. |
| Ship at sailing, ordered for a short expedition | **One month's stores** ("ready for sea with one month's stores"): Pocahontas, Pawnee and Harriet Lane, orders of 29-30 Mar 1861 (ORN I-4 pp.228-229). Buchanan to Rowan 1 Apr: Pawnee "ready to proceed to sea by the 6th instant, with one month's stores" (p.229). Welles to Rowan 4 Apr: Pawnee to Norfolk "for the purpose of receiving a month's supply of provisions" (p.234) | REQUIREMENT in the order | ORN I-4 pp.228-229, 234 | return | These are orders for the Sumter expedition, not a norm. They show one month was what the Department asked for a short cruise. |
| Ship, chartered or improvised | Keystone State, chartered 19 Apr 1861: "two weeks' provisions" and "seven days' coal" (ORN I-4 p.284, Du Pont). Baltic chartered "for one month" (p.245, Fox, 19 Apr) | PRACTICE | ORN I-4 pp.245, 284 | return | |
| Ship on station | Brooklyn off Pickens: "fifty days' provisions" and coal for eight days (19 Feb 1861, Adams, ORN I-4 p.85); "three weeks' (twenty-one days) provisions on hand for the crew, officers, and troops" (16 Mar, Paymaster Looker, p.96) | PRACTICE | ORN I-4 pp.85, 96 | return | These are stocks on station, not at sailing. The drop from 50 days to 21 in 25 days is faster than one day a day (troops aboard). |
| Ship, coal and water | Steamers ran short of coal and water long before provisions: Brooklyn coal for 8 days (19 Feb) and 5 days (21 Mar, p.100); Mohawk water for 7 days (12 Apr, p.106) and 2 days (19 Apr, p.139); Powhatan and Brooklyn "but a week's supply" of water left Pensacola (pp.181-182); Powhatan "fifty-four days under steam" (p.193) | PRACTICE | ORN I-4 pp.85, 100, 106, 139, 181-182, 193 | return | The binding limit for a steamer was coal and water, 5 to 8 days, not provisions. |

"On a campaign, or on marches, or on board of transports" hard bread is one pound | REQUIREMENT | Army Regulations 1861, para 1191 (p.243), para 1193 (p.243) | return | The footnote on p.243 raises the ration "during the rebellion in the Southern States" (bread or flour to 22 oz, hard bread to 1 lb). Whether that footnote belongs to the 1861 printing or the 1862/63 printings I did not establish. |
| Ration weight, one man one day | **about 2.0 lb net** (my sum of the para 1191 items with pork and hard bread, dry beans, and the per-100 items) to **2.5 lb** with packaging (Schneider, "Barrels, Boxes, and Bags", libertyrifles.org, 2.5 lb a man, 250,000 lb a day for 100,000 men) | inferred / secondary | My arithmetic from para 1191; Schneider, secondary, cites the 1861 Regulations and How to Feed an Army (1901) | inferred | No primary weight found. Package weights, all secondary: pork barrel 200 lb net, flour barrel 196 lb, hard-bread box 50 lb. |
| Confederate side | The CS ration followed the US model (secondary, not verified against a CS 1861 text). Reductions came later: April 1862 beef 1 lb or pork/bacon 1/2 lb; June 1863 1/2 lb for troops in movement, 1/3 lb stationary | PRACTICE | Northrop, Regulations of Subsistence Department other than those in Army Regulations (circulars Jan 1862 to Sep 1863), archive.org regulationsofsub00conf | return (1862-63, outside our dates) | No CS stocking norm, garrison or field, found for 1861. Northrop's circulars set no stock period. |

### 2. Worked reading for Sumter (what the garrison counts imply)

All inferred, not sourced:
- Hall's 16 barrels of flour lasting 19 days, at 196 lb a barrel (secondary weight) and 18 oz a ration, imply about 147 rations a day. His 31 barrels of pork lasting 2 1/4 months (about 68 days), at 200 lb a barrel and 12 oz a ration, imply about 120.
- So the stock-implied command on 1 Mar is about 120 to 150 men, against Scott's "less than 100 men, including common laborers and musicians" (12 Mar). Barrel weights are secondary, so treat this as a consistency check only.
- Meaning for the rule: a post's stores should be expressed per item (bread days and meat days), not one number; the shorter item sets the date.

### 3. Kinds with no norm found (these stay unknown)

- Fort Monroe, Fort Jefferson (Tortugas), Texas posts, Pacific posts, arsenals and depots: no stock figure for any date in 1861. Fort Monroe: only an order of 30 Apr 1861 saying Dimick had "a large amount of army subsistence stores" on hand, which gives no figure (search snippet, unverified). Fort Jefferson: one secondary mention of six months' provisions on the steamer Webster (Florida Keys History Center, unverified, no number carried).
- The Commissary General's annual report for 1860 or 1861 (Gibson, Taylor) and the Secretary of War's reports in the 36th/37th Congress documents: not located online by the search tool, so nothing from them. Archive.org copies of those reports would need a direct search.
- Union and Confederate regiments in training camps, militia, volunteer units on arrival: no norm found (no source on what they carried on arrival).
- Confederate garrisons and Confederate field forces for March to July 1861: no figure for stocks or carry norms.
- Navy: how many months' provisions a ship was *required* to take at sailing: not found. The 1857/1860 Navy Regulations were not on archive.org as full text, and HathiTrust is blocked. Only orders and observed stocks.
- Weight of one ration from a primary source: not found.

### 4. Gaps (needs-source) and blocked URLs

- Regulations for the Government of the Navy, 1857 or 1860 edition: needs a full-text copy (HathiTrust refuses automated requests; not tried). Google Books bub_gb_vY0DAAAAYAAJ on archive.org is the 1876-77 edition, not the one wanted.
- Commissary General's annual report 1860/1861: needs source.
- Patterson 9 Jul 1861: OR I-2 page number not shown on the mirror. Sumter 1 Apr 1861 (OR I-1 p.230): read only as a snippet. Vogdes's letter at OR I-1 pp.363-364: date, place and addressee not seen.
- Hall's inventory and Scott's 12 Mar reply are Lincoln Papers items, not in the OR (stated on the jfepperson.org page). The LoC item pages were not opened (LoC shows a bot check).

### 5. Conflicts

- **Sumter, March 1861:** Hall (1 Mar): bread 28 days, pork 2 1/4 months (about 68 days). Scott (12 Mar): bread, flour and rice about 26 days, pork about 48 days. Anderson's cover letter of 28 Feb / 2 Mar is described by secondary pages as "four to six weeks" (Tulane page, which gives no OR page; unverified wording). The three give a spread of about 4 to 6 weeks for bread and 7 to 10 weeks for meat.
- **Pickens reinforcement:** Thomas's letter of 21 Jan says up to four months; Dimick's order of 23 Jan says three months, as much as the Brooklyn could take. Both are in OR I-1 pp.352-53; the page does not explain.
- **Wagon load:** Bull Run trains give about 1,150-1,214 rations a wagon (OR I-2 pp.337, 343), while Van Vliet's 1862 figure (150 loads for 100,000 men a day) gives about 670. The first is of the principal parts of the ration only (OR I-2 p.343); the second is an 1862 planning figure that may include more than rations. Not reconciled.
- **Garrison size at Sumter:** stock-implied about 120-150 (inferred) against Scott's "less than 100".
- **Brooklyn provisions:** 50 days on 19 Feb 1861 to 21 days on 16 Mar (25 days): consumption of about 1.2 days a day, consistent with troops being aboard. Not a conflict between sources.
- **Army ration:** para 1191 gives hard bread 12 oz but para 1193 says 1 lb on a campaign or on board transports. This is inside one text, not between sources; use 12 oz in garrison and 1 lb on the march.

### 6. Findings outside scope

- The 1861 Army Regulations printing I could open carries a footnote raising the ration "during the rebellion"; this edition is the "with a full index" 1862 printing, and the 1863 appendix edition exists on archive.org as revisedunitedsta00unitrich. Which printing carries the footnote, and from what date, is needed before using the 22-oz figure for 1861.
- ORN I-4 p.202 says the St. Louis was fitted for a two-year cruise; if the pack has ship blocks for the Gulf squadron, this is the upper end of "at sailing".
- Navy ration, Act of 29 Aug 1842, 5 Stat. 546-547 (read on navycs.com, a transcription, not govinfo): bread 14 oz; salt pork 1 lb or salt beef 1 lb; 1 gill of spirits; coffee 1 oz; sugar 2 oz. Not checked against the Statutes at Large page. Useful only if the pack models ship consumption.
- The jfepperson.org pages reproduce Lincoln Papers items that are not in the OR; they carry the Sumter stock counts and are the best source for the 28-day figure.
- Confederate Subsistence Department created by the Act of 26 Feb 1861 (from a search summary, unverified).


## Appendix L: Union strengths from the Army table (helper N5b)


Snapshot date of the blocks: about 4-5 March 1861. Every strength below is a BAND built from sourced inputs plus arithmetic, except St Louis, where Harney and Hagner returns exist two weeks earlier. Nothing here is a return for 5 March.

### Method and caveats (read first)
- The fetch tool paraphrases and the civilwar.com OCR of OR III-1 pp.22-26 is scrambled. I read each page once or twice; two readings differed by up to 2 companies per department (e.g. Humboldt B or B+E 6th Inf; Colville 3 or 4 companies; Alcatraz H/3rd Art listed twice). Company counts below are therefore "my count of the printed list, +-2". Page numbers follow OCR running heads and may drift by one. No quotation below is claimed verbatim.
- Blocked in this run (not routed around): usmcu.edu (403, the Marine Corps History 2021 article and the Harper's Ferry pamphlet), history.navy.mil DANFS (connection refused), census.gov not tried. Shell network access (curl to archive.org) hung; I used WebFetch only.
- Measures. OR III-1 p.22 prints three columns per arm: "present" (officers/men/aggregate), "absent", and "present and absent" (the rolls). I use: PRESENT = officers + men present; ROLLS = present and absent. "Present for duty" (the June 1861 department returns) is lower than "aggregate present": for both Pacific and New Mexico it is 78 percent of aggregate present (2,178/2,805 and 1,518/1,941).

### Yardsticks used for every Army band (the common arithmetic)

Source A. OR III-1 p.22, "Abstract from returns of the U.S. Army, December 31, 1860" (page title read: civilwar.com id 268672; two readings, with OCR flags on several digits):

| Arm | Present: officers / men / aggregate | Absent: off / men / agg | Rolls (present+absent): off / men / agg |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cavalry (incl. Dragoons, Mounted Rifles) | 82 / 3,123 (3,125 in one reading) / 3,205 | 100 / 482 / 582 | 182 / 3,605 (3,607) / 3,787 |
| Artillery | 126 / 2,855 / 2,981 | 84 / 104 / 188 | 210 / 2,959 (2,955) / 3,169 (3,165) |
| Infantry | 164 / 7,140 (7,138) / 7,304 | 187 / 709 / 896 | 351 / 7,849 / 8,200 |
| Total Army | 745 / 13,918 / 14,663 | 371 / 1,341 (1,348) / 1,712 | 1,108 / 15,259 / 16,367 (16,382 in the other reading; components sum about 16,375) |

Both readings agree on 14,663 present, 13,918 men present, and on 16,367 as the printed final aggregate in one reading.

- Company counts (assumption, not read as a printed total): cavalry-type 5 regiments x 10 = 50 (1st and 2nd Cavalry, 1st and 2nd Dragoons, Mounted Rifles; company letters up to K appear in OR III-1 pp.24-26); infantry 10 regiments x 10 = 100; artillery 4 regiments x 12 = 48 (letter M appears for 1st and 3rd Artillery on pp.24-25). I did not read Heitman or the Army Register for company counts: flag this.
- Men per company, present: cavalry 3,123/50 = 62.5; infantry 7,140/100 = 71.4; artillery 2,855/48 = 59.5.
- Men per company, on the rolls: cavalry 3,605/50 = 72.1; infantry 7,849/100 = 78.5; artillery 2,959/48 = 61.6.
- Officers per company present: (82+126+164)/198 = 1.9; on the rolls (182+210+351)/198 = 3.75.

Authorized company size (law). 9 Stat. 439 (Act of 17 June 1850, sec. 2, page read via govinfo, PDF text layer): the President may enlist privates for companies serving "on the western frontier" and at "remote and distant stations" to a number not exceeding seventy-four (page 438 with the first half of the sentence not read). The same limits as summarised by the report printed at OR III-5 p.128 (read by paraphrase): old infantry and artillery regiments 42 privates a company (Act of 23 Aug 1842), up to 74 at frontier and remote stations (Act of 17 June 1850); the 8 light artillery companies 64 privates; new infantry 82 (Act of 29 July 1861); 5th Artillery 122 (29 July 1861); cavalry 78 (17 July 1862). Dragoons and Mounted Rifles are not given on that page. Quality: statute page 439 read (primary); the other figures secondary, via a later official compilation. The company's non-privates are not stated in anything I read for the regular service. The 1861 Army Regulations (suvcw.org PDF, text extracted) give no company table; para. 81 only says the artillery band at the Artillery School, Fort Monroe, is apportioned among the companies serving at the post.

Authorized and actual aggregate of the whole Army, to answer the lead's lead:
- The figure 16,367 is NOT an authorized strength. In OR III-1 p.22 it is the printed total of "present and absent" (the rolls) on 31 Dec 1860 (see table; components sum slightly differently). The present figure is 14,663 aggregate / 13,918 men. A search summary of a blog says "16,367 of an authorized 18,093": snippet-unverified, not used.
- OR III-1 p.700 (report of late 1861; read by paraphrase, author and date not visible on the page): "16,006 regulars" at the start of the rebellion (April 1861). Search summary of Eicher: 16,403 on paper and 14,658 present on 1 Jan 1861: snippet-unverified.
- OR III-1 p.699: estimated regular strength including the July 1861 act's enlistments, 20,304 or 20,334 (arm table: infantry 11,175, cavalry 4,744, artillery 4,308, engineers 107). After the window; for orientation only.
- Authorized legal maximum (Act of 1855 and 1861 acts): not found in a page I read. Gap.

### Ids still unsourced after this pass
- fb_us_ship_michigan and fb_us_ship_anacostia (only an unverified design complement; the 1861 Navy Register has no crew column; ORN I-1 4 March 1861 list of vessels in commission, which the repo notes lacks both vessels, has the crew column the other ships use).

- All of the Army blocks above are inferred bands except the arsenal. New Mexico and Pacific have a 30 June return, 17 weeks after the start.

### Conflicts
- 16,367: "authorized" in the lead, but OR III-1 p.22 prints it as present and absent (rolls). The only "authorized" total I met (18,093) is from a blog summary: snippet-unverified.
- Regular Army at the start: OR III-1 p.22: 14,663 present, 16,367 rolls (31 Dec 1860); OR III-1 p.700: 16,006 regulars at April 1861 (late-1861 report); Eicher via search summary: 16,403 paper, 14,658 present (1 Jan). Same order of magnitude; no clash if p.700 is on the rolls.
- New Mexico: arithmetic present 2,510 vs 1,941 (30 Jun aggregate present). Pacific: 3,130 vs 2,805. I assume attrition and detachments; unverified.
- Fort Monroe 3rd Artillery letters: F and K (both readings of OR III-1 p.23 and the 22 Jan order) vs I and K (the repo's earlier note): unresolved.
- Washington marines: 150 (sum of April fragments, partly secondary) vs 300 to 400 (secondary, snippet).
- St Louis: 493 (Harney, 19 Feb) vs 488 (Hagner, 21 Feb).
- Company counts from p.24 differ by up to 2 between my two readings (Colville 3 or 4, Humboldt 1 or 2).

### Gaps (needs-source) and blocked URLs
- Marine station figures and the 2 Nov 1860 report: https://www.usmcu.edu/Outreach/Marine-Corps-University-Press/MCH/Marine-Corps-History-Winter-2021/Understanding-Battlefield-Performance-of-US-Marines-Ashore-during-the-Civil-War/ (403); https://www.usmcu.edu/Portals/218/United%20States%20Marines%20at%20Harper_s%20Ferry%20and%20in%20the%20Civil%20War%20PCN%2019000410300.pdf (not fetched; blocked earlier); the Secretary of the Navy's 1860 and 1861 reports (not found online in a readable form: govinfo SERIALSET-00576 PDF exceeded the fetch size limit; SERIALSET-01048 not tried).
- DANFS Michigan I and Anacostia I: https://history.navy.mil/research/histories/ship-histories/danfs/m/michigan-i.html and .../a/anacostia-i.html (connection refused).
- ORN I-4 pp.283-285, 292 (marines at Philadelphia and Washington) not read; the repo's reading used.
- OR I-4 or OR I-50 pt 1 returns for Jan and Feb 1861 (Pacific and New Mexico): none found; the 31 Dec 1861 return exists at OR I-4 p.81.
- OR III-1 p.301 (30 Jun 1861 abstract) and OR I-1 p.655 (Hagner) were not re-fetched here.
- The company counts need a clean page image of OR III-1 pp.23-26, or Heitman and the Army Register for stations.
- Authorized legal company size for dragoons and Mounted Rifles, and for non-privates of regular companies, not found; 9 Stat. 438 (first half of the 17 June 1850 section 2) not read.

### Findings outside scope
- OR I-51 pt 1 pp.312-316 (civilwar.com ids 253660-253664) hold Washington security documents of Jan to Mar 1861: Scott's 24 Jan memo; Toucey, Harris and Taylor on Fort Washington; Scott's 12 Feb order to move marines to Capitol Square; Orders Nos. 61 and 62 of the Artillery Battalion in Washington (2 Mar: the batteries named Griffin, Barry, Fry, Elzey's and Haskin's companies, the West Point detachment of sappers and miners, the dragoons). None gives numbers but they list the Washington garrison units for 4 March.
- OR III-1 p.699 and 700: the 1861 estimate of regulars by arm and "16,006 regulars" at the start of the rebellion.
- No fetched page tried to give instructions to me.
